Like, I’ll finish one thing at school—I’m a special education teacher—and I immediately jump into the next thing without even taking a breath. Like yesterday, I finished my student reports and just immediately started reorganizing the sensory room. 

Which, by the way, the district just sent us these new beanbag chairs that are completely the wrong material. They’re made of this weird, scratchy nylon. I told the principal, you can't put scratchy nylon in a sensory room for these kids, but you know how administration is, they just look at the price tag. It’s exactly like when I went to buy a new lawnmower last weekend, the salesman tried to push the absolute cheapest model on me, but I know engines, you know? You get what you pay for. 

But anyway, yeah, I do it at home, too. I finish the lawn, I immediately start painting the fence, I finish the fence, I'm up on the ladder cleaning the gutters. I just... I jump from one thing to the next, and I can never seem to shut myself off.

Well, no, it hasn't always been *this* intense. I've always been an active guy, but this level of just... constantly running on overdrive? That really started about eight months ago. That's when our older relative, my wife's uncle, moved in with us. 

Which, let me tell you, was a massive transition. We had to completely clear out the downstairs study for him, which I had actually been using to store my fishing gear and this antique radio I was restoring. Beautiful piece, a 1930s Philco. The vacuum tubes were completely shot, and finding replacement parts for those things is practically a part-time job. I was in this intense bidding war on eBay with some guy in Ohio for an original dial bezel. I swear people on there just use bots to bid at the last second. 

But anyway, Uncle Arthur moved in because he couldn't manage his stairs anymore, so we had to buy him this heavy-duty adjustable bed. The delivery guys just dumped the boxes right on the front porch and refused to bring them inside. Can you believe that? The nerve of some companies today. I ended up dragging it all in and putting the entire motorized frame together myself in like forty-five minutes. But yeah, ever since he got to our house eight months ago, it's just been non-stop.

Sleep? Well, I mean, I guess I'm still getting my usual amount, maybe six or seven hours? I haven't really noticed a change there. I still hit the pillow and I'm out, mostly. But man, getting Uncle Arthur settled for the night is a whole process. 

He insists on watching those old westerns on maximum volume right up until bedtime. *Gunsmoke*, *Bonanza*, you name it. And the volume on that TV... I swear the neighbors three houses down can hear every gunshot. I tried getting him those wireless headphones, you know, the ones they advertise for seniors? The pairing process on those things is ridiculous. You have to hold down two buttons while waiting for a blue light to flash, but it always flashes red instead, which means it's trying to connect to my wife's iPad in the kitchen. Bluetooth technology is supposed to make things easier, but I spend half my life just trying to get devices to talk to each other. 

It's like the smartboard in my classroom. The district spent thousands on these interactive boards, and the calibration is always off by like an inch. Try teaching a kid with fine motor delays how to trace a letter when the line appears an inch below where they're touching. It defeats the entire purpose! I spent three hours on the phone with IT last week, and they just told me to restart it. Restart it! As if I hadn't thought of that. 

But yeah, sleep-wise, I think I'm getting about the same as I always have.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Euphoric? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not walking around on cloud nine or anything. Just... busy, you know? Just always moving. 

But honestly, who has time to be euphoric these days? Have you seen the price of groceries? I went to the store yesterday just to pick up some coffee and a carton of eggs for Uncle Arthur—he will only eat the brown eggs, mind you. He claims the white ones taste like cardboard, which makes absolutely no sense because the color of the shell just depends on the breed of the chicken. I actually read this whole article about it once, about Rhode Island Reds versus Leghorns. It's fascinating how genetics dictate egg color. 

Anyway, I get to the checkout, and the total for just those two things was almost twelve dollars! Twelve dollars! The cashier—this kid who looked like he was barely out of middle school, wearing his apron backward for some reason—just shrugged at me. I tried to explain to him that back when I was his age working at the Piggly Wiggly, we actually had to memorize the produce codes. We didn't have these fancy scanners that do all the work for you. You had to know that bananas were 4011. If you didn't know 4011, you didn't last a week. 

But yeah, no, I'm not feeling overly cheerful or anything like that. Just my normal mood, I guess.

I actually read this whole article about it once, about Rhode Island Reds versus Leghorns. It's fascinating how genetics dictate egg color. \n\nAnyway, I get to the checkout, and the total for just those two things was almost twelve dollars! Twelve dollars! Talking faster? I don't think so. No one's really said anything about it. I mean, my wife tells me to slow down sometimes, but she's always said that. She's from Georgia, you know, they do everything a little slower down there. 

We went down to visit her sister in Savannah last summer, and I swear, just ordering a sweet tea at a restaurant takes twenty minutes. Beautiful city, though. The Spanish moss on those old oak trees is really something. But the humidity! It's like walking around in a bowl of soup. I had to change my shirt three times a day. I brought this special moisture-wicking fabric shirt that I bought for hiking, but even that couldn't handle the Savannah heat. 

I actually got into a whole conversation with this tour guide about the architectural history of the historic district. Did you know they used ballast stones from the old ships to pave the streets down by the river? It's fascinating. But walking on those cobblestones in regular sneakers is a nightmare for your arches. I had to buy these special orthotic inserts when we got back. The podiatrist told me my left arch is collapsing faster than my right one, which is weird because I'm right-handed. You'd think the dominant side would take more wear and tear, right? 

But anyway, no, nobody's really mentioned me talking faster than normal. I'm just a fast talker anyway, being from up north. It's just how we communicate.

I swear people on there just use bots to bid at the last second. \n\nBut anyway, Uncle Arthur moved in because he couldn't manage his stairs anymore, so we had to buy him this heavy-duty adjustable bed. You had to know that bananas were 4011. If you didn't know 4011, you didn't last a week. \n\nBut yeah, no, I'm not feeling overly cheerful or anything like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, as a kid? Yeah, definitely. This isn't some new thing that just started when Uncle Arthur moved in, if that's what you're thinking. My mother used to joke that even in elementary school, I was the one teaching the class because I could never just sit in my seat. I was always up, wandering around, talking, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. 

I remember Mrs. Gable in third grade—she had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every morning. Seriously, you could smell the Aqua Net from the hallway. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, but even then, I'd be up sharpening my pencil five times an hour. Those old crank sharpeners, you know? The ones bolted to the wall that would chew your pencil down to a nub if you weren't careful. I used to love the smell of those cedar shavings. 

Actually, I bought one of those vintage crank sharpeners for my classroom last year at a flea market. Paid twenty bucks for it. I thought the kids would get a kick out of it, maybe appreciate the tactile experience, you know? But they don't even use regular pencils anymore! It's all mechanical pencils with those tiny little leads that snap if you look at them wrong. I spend half my day sweeping up little bits of graphite from the floor. The custodian, Gary, he's always complaining about it. Gary's a good guy, though. He's been with the district for thirty years. He was telling me the other day about his pension plan, and honestly, I should have looked into the state retirement system earlier because the way things are going...

But yeah, the restlessness and getting sidetracked? That's just always been me. Long before adulthood, long before any of this. It's just how I've always been wired.

She's from Georgia, you know, they do everything a little slower down there. \n\nWe went down to visit her sister in Savannah last summer, and I swear, just ordering a sweet tea at a restaurant takes twenty minutes. Beautiful city, though. The Spanish moss on those old oak trees is really something. But the humidity! It's like walking around in a bowl of soup. I had to change my shirt three times a day. I was always up, wandering around, talking, cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. \n\nI remember Mrs. Gable in third grade—she had this huge beehive hairdo that must have taken a whole can of hairspray every morning. Seriously, you could smell the Aqua Net from the hallway. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, but even then, I'd be up sharpening my pencil five times an hour. I used to love the smell of those cedar shavings. \n\nActually, I bought one of those vintage crank sharpeners for my classroom last year at a flea market. Paid twenty bucks for it. I thought the kids would get a kick out of it, maybe appreciate the tactile experience, you know? But they don't even use regular pencils anymore! Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nSleep? Well, I mean, I guess I'm still getting my usual amount, maybe six or seven hours? I haven't really noticed a change there. I still hit the pillow and I'm out, mostly. But man, getting Uncle Arthur settled for the night is a whole process. \n\nHe insists on watching those old westerns on maximum volume right up until bedtime. *Gunsmoke*, *Bonanza*, you name it. And the volume on that TV... I swear the neighbors three houses down can hear every gunshot. I tried getting him those wireless headphones, you know, the ones they advertise for seniors? The pairing process on those things is ridiculous. You have to hold down two buttons while waiting for a blue light to flash, but it always flashes red instead, which means it's trying to connect to my wife's iPad in the kitchen. Bluetooth technology is supposed to make things easier, but I spend half my life just trying to get devices to talk to each other. \n\nIt's like the smartboard in my classroom. The district spent thousands on these interactive boards, and the calibration is always off by like an inch. Try teaching a kid with fine motor delays how to trace a letter when the line appears an inch below where they're touching. It defeats the entire purpose! I spent three hours on the phone with IT last week, and they just told me to restart it. Restart it! As if I hadn't thought of that. \n\nBut yeah, sleep-wise, I think I'm getting about the same as I always have.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nEuphoric? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not walking around on cloud nine or anything. Just... busy, you know? Just always moving. \n\nBut honestly, who has time to be euphoric these days? Have you seen the price of groceries? I went to the store yesterday just to pick up some coffee and a carton of eggs for Uncle Arthur—he will only eat the brown eggs, mind you. He claims the white ones taste like cardboard, which makes absolutely no sense because the color of the shell just depends on the breed of the chicken. I actually read this whole article about it once, about Rhode Island Reds versus Leghorns. It's fascinating how genetics dictate egg color. \n\nAnyway, I get to the checkout, and the total for just those two things was almost twelve dollars! Twelve dollars! The cashier—this kid who looked like he was barely out of middle school, wearing his apron backward for some reason—just shrugged at me. I tried to explain to him that back when I was his age working at the Piggly Wiggly, we actually had to memorize the produce codes. We didn't have these fancy scanners that do all the work for you. You had to know that bananas were 4011. If you didn't know 4011, you didn't last a week. \n\nBut yeah, no, I'm not feeling overly cheerful or anything like that. Just my normal mood, I guess.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nTalking faster? I don't think so. No one's really said anything about it. I mean, my wife tells me to slow down sometimes, but she's always said that. She's from Georgia, you know, they do everything a little slower down there. \n\nWe went down to visit her sister in Savannah last summer, and I swear, just ordering a sweet tea at a restaurant takes twenty minutes. Beautiful city, though. The Spanish moss on those old oak trees is really something. But the humidity! It's like walking around in a bowl of soup. I had to change my shirt three times a day. I brought this special moisture-wicking fabric shirt that I bought for hiking, but even that couldn't handle the Savannah heat. \n\nI actually got into a whole conversation with this tour guide about the architectural history of the historic district. Did you know they used ballast stones from the old ships to pave the streets down by the river? It's fascinating. But walking on those cobblestones in regular sneakers is a nightmare for your arches. I had to buy these special orthotic inserts when we got back. The podiatrist told me my left arch is collapsing faster than my right one, which is weird because I'm right-handed. You'd think the dominant side would take more wear and tear, right? \n\nBut anyway, no, nobody's really mentioned me talking faster than normal. I'm just a fast talker anyway, being from up north. It's just how we communicate.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
